ALG gravitational instantons and Hitchin moduli spaces, I: Torelli parameters
Laura Fredrickson, Rafe Mazzeo, Jan Swoboda, Hartmut Weiss

Abstract
. This is the first of two papers which together prove that the 12 12 -parameter family of parabolic S ​ U ​ ( 2 ) SU(2) -Hitchin moduli spaces on the four-punctured sphere are all ALG gravitational instantons of type D4, and hence are asymptotic to ( ℂ × T τ 2 ) / ℤ 2 (\mathbb{C}\times T^{2}_{\tau})/\mathbb{Z}_{2} at infinity. The elliptic modulus τ \tau is determined by the cross-ratio of the four points. In this first paper, we consider each Hitchin moduli space corresponding to an allowable set of parabolic data and compute its Torelli parameters. There is a 12 12 -parameter family of Hitchin moduli spaces corresponding to different parabolic data, and we show that these realize all possible allowable Torelli parameters. In the companion paper, we we will show there that all of the Hitchin moduli spaces studied here are indeed ALG of type D 4 D_{4} , and consequently that every ALG- D 4 D_{4} gravitational instanton can be realized as a Hitchin moduli space. Altogether, this will give the first verification of any case of the Modularity Conjecture: that all ALG gravitational instantons with tangent cone ℂ / ℤ 2 \mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}_{2} can be realized as Hitchin moduli spaces with their natural associated L 2 L^{2} metrics.



August 10, 2026

1.
Introduction

Let
C
be a compact Riemann surface and
D=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{N}\}
a divisor on
C
with all points of multiplicity
1
. Let
E
be a rank
2

complex vector bundle over
C
. We shall consider parabolic structures on
(C,D,E)
, consisting of the following data: a full flag

\{0\}\subset F_{p_{i}}\subset E_{p_{i}}:=E|_{p_{i}}
in the fiber of
E
over each point
p_{i}
in
D
, as well as an assignment of two real numbers

\alpha^{(1)}(p_{i}),\alpha^{(2)}(p_{i})\in(0,1)
and a complex number
m_{i}
for each
p_{i}\in D
. In general, the bundle
E
may have higher rank, one does not
require the flag to be full, and the numbers
\alpha^{(i)}(p)
are unrelated. In this paper we specialize by requiring
E
to be rank
2
, as already stated,
and to have structure group
\mathrm{SU}(2)
, which is equivalent to fixing a Hermitian metric on
\det E
and requiring all transformations below
to be compatible with this choice. In particular, this forces that
\alpha^{(1)}(p_{i})+\alpha^{(2)}(p_{i})=1
, so it suffices to specify only

\alpha^{(1)}(p_{i})\in(0,1/2)
, which we call simply
\alpha_{i}
for simplicity.

Any such choice of parabolic data leads to three seemingly distinct collections of objects: a space of compatible Higgs bundles, a space of gauge fields
(A,\varphi)

which solve the Hitchin equations and have specified singularities at points of
D
, and a space of flat
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
connections on
C^{\times}=C\setminus D
, again with specified holonomy and singular behavior at points of
D
. There are gauge groups acting on each of these spaces. The first main theorem
in this subject is that restricting to suitable subsets of stable objects in each space, the quotients of each of these spaces
by the relevant gauge group defines a smooth moduli space, and the three moduli spaces determined in this way are mutually diffeomorphic, but inherit
different complex structures. These identifications, in various directions, are called the Kobayashi-Hitchin and nonlinear Hodge correspondences. The
moduli space itself is denoted
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
, where
\bm{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{N})
and
\mathbf{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{N})
.

In slightly more detail, a Higgs bundle consists of a pair
({\mathcal{E}},\varphi)
where
{\mathcal{E}}=(E,\overline{\partial}_{A})
is a holomorphic structure on
E
and
\varphi
is a
meromorphic section of
\mathrm{Hom}({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{E}}\otimes K_{C})
with simple poles at
D
. The ‘real mass parameters’
\alpha_{j}
in this setting are extra data
whose role will be explained later, while the ‘complex mass parameters’
m_{j}
specify the eigenvalues of the residue of
\varphi
at
p_{j}
. Next, the gauge field
A
is a unitary
connection on
E
and
\varphi
is a section of
\mathrm{Hom}\,(E,E\otimes K_{C})
; each has first order blowup at the points of
D
with singular part
determined by the
\alpha_{j}
and
m_{j}
, respectively. These satisfy the Hitchin equations

(1.1)

F_{A}^{\perp}+[\varphi,\varphi^{*}]=0,\ \ \overline{{\partial}}_{A}\varphi=0,

where the adjoint is taken with respect to a fixed Hermitian metric
h_{0}
on
E
. An alternate point of view is that if we begin with the holomorphic structure

\overline{\partial}_{A}
, then we seek a Hermitian metric
h_{0}
such that the system (
1.1
) is satisfied, where
F_{A}
is the curvature of the associated Chern
connection
\overline{\partial}_{A}+\partial_{A}^{h_{0}}
. Finally, in terms of these fields, the flat
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
connections
we consider take the form
\nabla_{A}+\zeta^{-1}\varphi+\zeta\varphi^{*}
for
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. The group
\mathcal{G}
of unitary gauge transformations acts on solutions of the Hitchin equations and
on the space of flat connections; its complexification
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
acts on the space of Higgs bundles.

In this paper we shall primarily focus on the space of pairs
(A,\varphi)
which solve the Hitchin equations from the Higgs bundle perspective.
It is known that

(1.2)

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})=6(\mathrm{genus}(C)-1)+2N.

There is a natural hyperKähler metric on the affine space of all pairs
(A,\varphi)
, and an infinite dimensional
version of hyperKähler reduction defines a metric
g
on this quotient moduli space. Another key feature of this moduli space is the existence
of the Hitchin fibration
\mathrm{Hit}
, which maps a pair
({\mathcal{E}},\varphi)
(or equivalently,
(A,\varphi)
) to
-\det\varphi
, which is a meromorphic quadratic differential
on
C
, with poles of order no more than
2
at points of
D
. The leading singular terms of these differentials are determined by the
complex masses
m_{j}
. The range of
\mathrm{Hit}
is an affine space, and this map is known to be surjective and proper.

While much is known about the geometry and topology of the hyperKähler spaces
(\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m}),g)
, many open questions remain. Our goal here is to
study some of these questions when
C=\mathbb{CP}^{1}

and
|D|=4
. In this special case,
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})=2
; note that by (
1.2
), the only other case where the moduli space has this dimension
is when
g=N=1
, i.e., for the once-punctured torus
1
1

1

There is a
3
-parameter family of Hitchin moduli spaces on once-punctured torus. It is expected that each of these hyperKähler structures can also be obtained from a
3
-parameter subfamily of Hitchin moduli spaces on the four-punctured sphere.
. By one of the general results obtained by Hitchin in his initial investigation of these spaces
[
Hit87
]
(see
[
Sim90
]

for this result in the parabolic case), the metric
g_{L^{2}}
is complete. In other words,
(\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m}),g_{L^{2}})
is a complete, noncompact (real)
four-dimensional hyperKähler space. It is expected that the Riemannian curvature tensor is
L^{2}
-integrable. We will prove this in the companion paper.

Gravitational Instantons:
Complete, noncompact (real) four-dimensional hyperKähler spaces with integrable Riemannian curvature tensors are known as
gravitational instantons
, and are fundamental objects in the study of four-dimensional
Riemannian manifolds with special holonomy.
Gravitational instantons have been the focus of intensive study, particularly in the last decade, and there is now a fairly complete classification. There are six
different types: ALE, ALF, ALG
∗
, ALG, ALH
∗
, and ALH,
based on their asymptotic geometric structure, and within each type the possible topological types are classified by affine Dynkin
diagrams (which correspond to their intersection forms on middle degree integer homology). Finally, fixing both the asymptotic and
topological type, there are still continuous moduli. The monikers ALE and ALF are acronyms for asymptotically locally Euclidean and
asymptotically locally flat; the remaining ones, ALG
∗
/G/H
∗
/H, were coined by S. Cherkis (with
∗
additions by Hein),
as the natural ‘continuation’ of this series. The most ‘visible’ geometric differences between these spaces are based on their
volume and quasi-isometry classes. Thus, the ALE spaces are asymptotically conical; ALF spaces are
S^{1}
fibrations over three-dimensional
asymptotically conical spaces, where the
S^{1}
fibers degenerate at some finite collection of points and which have lengths converging to
some finite positive number at infinity; ALG and ALG
∗
spaces are
T^{2}
fibrations over two-dimensional asymptotically conical spaces,
with the
T^{2}
fibers degenerating at some finite collection of points and with the fiber metrics converging to a flat metric on
T^{2}
of fixed
area in the ALG case but asymptotic to a diverging family of flat conformal structures on
T^{2}
in the ALG
∗
case; similarly, the
ALH and ALH
∗
spaces are fibrations over a half-line with fiber a three-dimensional nilmanifold, with similar convergence and divergence
properties in the unstarred and starred cases, respectively. There has been significant progress in understanding these gravitational
instanton moduli spaces, starting from the celebrated thesis of Kronheimer
[
Kro89a
,
Kro89b
]
, where a complete classification of all ALE spaces was
obtained. That all gravitational instantons fall into one of these six classes is the upshot of a number of papers by several different authors, leading to the final resolution in
[
SZ24
]
. The classification of ALG and ALG
∗

starts with G. Chen-X. Chen
[
CC15
,
CC16
]
.
In the companion paper we shall provide a much more detailed description of this entire classification scheme.

Fixing
\bm{\alpha}
and
\mathbf{m}
, the Hitchin moduli space
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
for the four-punctured sphere is a singular
T^{2}
fibration over a two real
dimensional (asymptotically) conical space. The map to this base is the Hitchin fibration. Because of this, it is natural to suspect that these parabolic
Hitchin moduli spaces are either ALG or ALG
∗
.

The goal of
this paper and its companion
is to prove that these are in fact always ALG:

Theorem 1.1
.

As the parabolic parameters
\bm{\alpha}
,
\mathbf{m}
vary, the spaces
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
nearly exhaust the entire family of ALG spaces with affine D4
topology.

This directly answers one case of the so-called ‘Modularity Conjecture’, articulated by Boalch,
which states loosely that every possible
gravitational instanton occurs as a gauge theoretic moduli space. In particular,

Conjecture 1.2

(Modularity Conjecture
[
Boa
]
)
.

Every ALG or ALG
∗
gravitational instanton arises as the hyperKähler metric on some Hitchin moduli space.

Our result will be the first of this kind for ALG
∗
/G/H
∗
/H moduli spaces, where the proof is considerably harder than the ALE or ALF cases.

In this particular case, the Torelli Theorem of
[
CVZ24
]
[Theorem 1.10] states that marked ALG-
D_{4}
gravitational are classified by the classes of
[\omega_{I}],[\omega_{J}],[\omega_{K}]
, or alternatively, the integrals of
\omega_{I},\Omega_{I}=\omega_{J}+i\omega_{K}
over a given basis of the homology. See Section
1.4
for a more precise statement.

In
this paper
, we prove two main results. These are not stated precisely, since there is considerable notation we must introduce first; but we refer to the precise statement appearing later.

Theorem 1.3

(cf.  Theorem
4.1
)
.

The class
[\omega_{I}]
is an certain affine linear function of
\bm{\alpha}
and the class
[\Omega_{I}=\omega_{J}+i\omega_{K}]
is a certain linear function of
\mathbf{m}
.

Theorem 1.4

(cf.  See Theorem
9.2
)
.

As the parabolic parameters
\bm{\alpha}
,
\mathbf{m}
vary, the classes of
[\omega_{I}],[\omega_{J}],[\omega_{K}]
nearly exhaust the allowable Torelli parameters of the family of ALG spaces with affine D4
topology.

We now address this annoying word “nearly”. The statement in Theorem
9.2
clarifies that the issue is that the set
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}
of extended parameters
(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
is a proper subset of
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathrm{full}}
(see Definition
9.1
). Fundamentally, this discrepancy is for the following reason: as
(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
approaches one of the points in
([0,\frac{1}{2}]^{4}\times\mathbb{C}^{4})\cap(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathrm{full}}\setminus\widetilde{\mathcal{R}})
, necessarily
\bm{\alpha}
is approaching the boundary
{\partial}[0,\frac{1}{2}]^{4}
and
\mathbf{m}\neq\mathbf{0}
; in this case we are degenerating from the full flag case to the not-full-flag case; in the current definition of the such weakly parabolic not-full-flags Higgs bundle moduli spaces, the dimension of these moduli spaces drops; the moduli spaces are not four-dimensional, and thus not ALG-
D_{4}
.

Conjecture 1.5
.

There is a more natural definition of the Higgs bundle moduli space when the flags are not all full, i.e. when for some
p_{i}
,
\alpha_{i}\in\{0,\frac{1}{2}\}
.

If Conjecture
1.5
holds, then we can remove the word “nearly” in Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
9.2
.

In the
companion paper
, we will prove the necessary result that the Riemannian curvature tensor is
L^{2}
-integrable, hence,

Theorem 1.6
.

The moduli space
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
is ALG for every generic choice
(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
.

This will extend our result that
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{0})
is ALG-
D_{4}
in
[
FMSW22
]
, a paper about asymptotic geometry of the moduli space of weakly parabolic
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs bundles. Theorem
1.1
follows from the above results and the Torelli Theorem of
[
CVZ24
]
[Theorem 1.10].

Proving such a result was considerably more involved than we originally expected. In the strongly parabolic case, it is easy to compute
[\omega_{I}]
. However, the weakly parabolic case is difficult. While we expected to show that
[\omega_{I}]
doesn’t depend on
\mathbf{m}
using some
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action, we could only control the integrals of
[\omega_{I}]
by considering the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action of the recently analytically constructed moduli space
\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})
in
[
CFW24
]
. (I.e. we had to justify that
\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m}
is a smooth parameter of the moduli space, and hence
[\omega_{I}]
depend smoothly on it.)
Similarly, while we compute
[\Omega_{I}]
via intersection numbers of some submanifolds, we again had to use the theory in
[
CFW24
]
to justify that these were submanifolds. In practice, we show that
[\Omega_{I}]
agrees with the semiflat holomorphic symplectic form in Gaiotto–Moore–Neitzke.
But for now we note that relevant case of ALG-
D_{4}
gravitational instantons are asymptotic to
(\mathbb{C}\times T^{2}_{\tau})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
for some torus parameter
\tau
.

1.1.
Fixed data for Hitchin moduli space

We now say more about the Higgs bundle moduli space on the four-punctured sphere. Since the Higgs bundle moduli space depends only on the complex structure
of
(\mathbb{CP}^{1},D)
, we may as well use a Möbius transformation to transform
D
to
\{0,1,\infty,p_{0}\}
in the extended complex plane. Fixing the first three points
removes the Möbius freedom, leaving
p_{0}
as a free parameter. This parameter determines the complex modulus
\tau
of the limiting torus fibers of the Hitchin
fibration by the equation
p_{0}=\lambda(\tau)
, where
\lambda
is the elliptic modular function. It is also possible to write the
j
-invariant of the elliptic curve
T_{\tau}
as a rational function of
p_{0}
. These explicit formulæ will be omitted
since they are not needed here.

As already noted, the parabolic parameters
\bm{\alpha}
and
\mathbf{m}
act as ‘boundary conditions’ at each
p_{j}
. To explain this, we use
the flag data
\{0\}\subset F_{j}\subset E_{p_{j}}
. The residue of the Higgs field is required to preserve these flags at each
p_{j}
and
acts by multiplication by
m_{j}
on
F_{j}
(and hence, by the trace-free condition, by
-m_{j}
on
{\mathcal{E}}_{p_{j}}/F_{j}
). Thus, the Hitchin fibration carries a
Higgs bundle
({\mathcal{E}},\varphi)
to the meromorphic quadratic differential
q=-\det\varphi=m_{j}^{2}z^{-2}dz^{2}+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1})
. If all
m_{j}=0
, which
is called the strongly parabolic case, these residues map
E_{p_{j}}\to F_{j}
and
F_{j}\to 0
, or in other words,
\varphi
has nilpotent residue at each
p_{j}
.

The role of the
\alpha_{j}
is less direct. In the Higgs bundle picture, these are simply additional parameters, but are subject to a stability condition. There is a notion of parabolic stability. The parabolic degree of the rank
2
Higgs bundle with given real mass parameters

\alpha^{(1)}(p_{j}),\alpha^{(2)}(p_{j})
at each
p_{j}
is, by definition,

\mathrm{pdeg}\,({\mathcal{E}}(\bm{\alpha}))=\mathrm{deg}\,({\mathcal{E}})+\sum(\alpha^{(1)}(p_{j})+\alpha^{(2)}(p_{j})).

Since
\alpha^{(1)}(p_{j})+\alpha^{(2)}(p_{j})=1
, this reduces to
\mathrm{deg}\,({\mathcal{E}})+4
, and we require in this paper that this parabolic degree vanishes.
We say that
({\mathcal{E}}(\bm{\alpha}),\varphi)
is
stable
if, for any holomorphic line subbundle
\mathcal{V}\subset{\mathcal{E}}
preserved by
\varphi
, one has

\mathrm{pdeg}\,(\mathcal{V}(\bm{\alpha}))=\mathrm{deg}\,(\mathcal{V})+\sum\alpha_{j}(\mathcal{V})<\mathrm{pdeg}\,({\mathcal{E}}(\bm{\alpha})),

where

\alpha_{j}(\mathcal{V})=\begin{cases}1-\alpha_{j}&V|_{p_{j}}=F_{j}\\
\alpha_{j}&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

We say that
({\mathcal{E}}(\bm{\alpha}),\varphi)
is
polystable
if it is a direct sum of two holomorphic line subbundles, each preserved by
\varphi
.
We say that
({\mathcal{E}}(\bm{\alpha}),\varphi)
is
semistable
if equality
\mathrm{pdeg}\,(\mathcal{V}(\bm{\alpha}))\leq\mathrm{pdeg}\,({\mathcal{E}}(\bm{\alpha}))
, and note that for generic choices of
(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
, semistability implies stability.

The parabolic parameters
\bm{\alpha}

are used directly, however, to define the correspondence with solutions to the Hitchin equations in (
1.1
). Indeed, first note that the flag determines a filtration
\mathcal{F}

of the space of germs of holomorphic sections of
{\mathcal{E}}
at
p_{j}
, namely
\mathcal{F}_{j,1}=\mathcal{O}\supset{\mathcal{E}}_{j,\alpha_{j}}\supset\mathcal{F}_{j,0}=\mathcal{O}(-1)
. Here,
\mathcal{O}(-1)
is the space of germs of holomorphic sections of
{\mathcal{E}}
vanishing to first order at
p_{j}
.
The quotient
\mathcal{F}_{j,1}/\mathcal{F}_{j,0}
is identified with the fiber
{\mathcal{E}}_{p_{j}}
, while by definition
{\mathcal{E}}_{j,\alpha_{j}}/\mathcal{F}_{j,0}
is the subspace
\mathcal{L}_{j}
. We say that
a Hermitian metric
h
on
{\mathcal{E}}
is admissible if, near
p_{j}
,
h=\mathcal{O}(|z|^{2(1-\alpha_{j})-\epsilon})
on the germs in
\mathcal{F}_{j,0}
and
h=\mathcal{O}(|z|^{2\alpha_{j}-\epsilon})

on
\mathcal{F}_{j,1}
, for any
\epsilon>0
.

The moduli space
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
is now defined to be the quotient of the space of all stable pairs
({\mathcal{E}},\varphi)
by the group of all smooth
\mathrm{SL}(2,E)

gauge transformations, or alternately, as the space of all solutions of (
1.1
) moduli the group of all smooth
\mathrm{SU}(2,E)
gauge transformations.
The aforementioned Kobayashi–Hitchin and nonlinear Hodge correspondences require, as a central hypothesis, this parabolic stability.

1.2.
HyperKähler preliminaries

Each moduli space
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
carries a natural
L^{2}
metric, which is always hyperKähler and complete.
It is most naturally defined on the moduli space of solutions of the Hitchin equations

\mathcal{M}=\{(\overline{\partial}_{A},\varphi):\overline{\partial}_{A}\varphi=0,F^{\perp}_{\overline{\partial}_{A}+{\partial}^{h_{0}}_{A}}+[\varphi,\varphi^{*_{h_{0}}}]=0\}/\mathcal{G}

on a fixed complex bundle
E\to C
with weighted flags at
p\in D
and adapted hermitian metric
h_{0}
, modulo gauge transformations preserving
h_{0}
.
Then, the tangent space at
(\overline{\partial}_{A},\varphi)
consists of deformations
(\dot{A}^{(0,1)},\dot{\varphi})
satisfying the linearized equations, modulo equivalence.
In each deformation class, there is a unique representative—the
harmonic representative
—which is perpendicular to the gauge orbit. This “Coulomb gauge” condition and the infinitesimal version of
F^{\perp}_{\overline{\partial}_{A}+{\partial}^{h_{0}}_{A}}+[\varphi,\varphi^{*_{h_{0}}}]=0
can be combined into a single equation

(1.3)

{\partial}_{A}^{h_{0}}\dot{A}^{0,1}+[\varphi^{*_{h_{0}}},\dot{\varphi}]=0.

The hyperKähler metric is naturally an
L^{2}
-metric on the harmonic representatives.

In particular, let
v_{i}=(\dot{A}_{i}^{0,1},\dot{\varphi}_{i})
be harmonic tangent vectors,
i=1,2
. If
z=x+iy
is a local holomorphic coordinate, the Kähler metric on
C
is given by
e^{2u}(dx^{2}+dy^{2})
, hence
dA=e^{2u}dx\wedge dy
. In particular
|dz|^{2}dA=|d\bar{z}|^{2}dA=2dx\wedge dy=idz\wedge d\bar{z}
.
The hermitian metric on the space of harmonic tangent vectors is

(1.4)

\displaystyle h(v_{1},v_{2})

\displaystyle=2\int_{C}\left(\langle\dot{A}_{1}^{0,1},\dot{A}_{2}^{0,1}\rangle+\langle\dot{\varphi}_{1},\dot{\varphi}_{2}\rangle\right)\,dA

(1.5)

\displaystyle=2i\int_{C}\Tr\left((\dot{A}_{2}^{0,1})^{*}\wedge\dot{A}_{1}^{0,1}+\dot{\varphi}_{1}\wedge(\dot{\varphi}_{2})^{*}\right)

and the associated Riemannian metric is

(1.6)

\displaystyle g(v_{1},v_{2})=\operatorname{Re}h(v_{1},v_{2})=-2\operatorname{Im}\int_{C}\Tr\left((\dot{A}_{2}^{0,1})^{*}\wedge\dot{A}_{1}^{0,1}+\dot{\varphi}_{1}\wedge(\dot{\varphi}_{2})^{*}\right).

The factor of
2
is explained above (
A.2.1
).
The complex structures are

\displaystyle I(\dot{A}^{0,1},\dot{\varphi})

\displaystyle=({\mathrm{i}}\dot{A}^{0,1},{\mathrm{i}}\dot{\varphi})

\displaystyle J(\dot{A}^{0,1},\dot{\varphi})

\displaystyle=(\dot{\varphi}^{*},-(\dot{A}^{0,1})^{*})

\displaystyle K(\dot{A}^{0,1},\dot{\varphi})

\displaystyle=({\mathrm{i}}\dot{\varphi}^{*},-{\mathrm{i}}(\dot{A}^{0,1})^{*}).

The
Kähler forms
2
2

2

Here we have used that for
\alpha=Adz\in\Omega^{1,0}
and
\beta=Bd\bar{z}\in\Omega^{0,1}
we have

\overline{\Tr\alpha\wedge\beta}=\overline{\Tr Adz\wedge Bd\bar{z}}=\Tr A^{*}B^{*}d\bar{z}\wedge dz=\Tr\alpha^{*}\wedge\beta^{*}=-\Tr\beta^{*}\wedge\alpha^{*}

and therefore
\operatorname{Re}\Tr\beta^{*}\wedge\alpha^{*}=-\operatorname{Re}\Tr\alpha\wedge\beta
and
\operatorname{Im}\Tr\beta^{*}\wedge\alpha^{*}=\operatorname{Im}\Tr\alpha\wedge\beta
.

\omega_{I_{j}}(v,w)=g(I_{j}v,w)
,
j=1,2,3
are

\displaystyle\omega_{I}(v_{1},v_{2})

\displaystyle=-2\operatorname{Im}\int_{C}\Tr\left((\dot{A}_{2}^{0,1})^{*}\wedge i\dot{A}_{1}^{0,1}+i\dot{\varphi}_{1}\wedge(\dot{\varphi}_{2})^{*}\right)=-2\operatorname{Re}\int_{C}\Tr\left((\dot{A}_{2}^{0,1})^{*}\wedge\dot{A}_{1}^{0,1}+\dot{\varphi}_{1}\wedge(\dot{\varphi}_{2})^{*}\right)

\displaystyle\omega_{J}(v_{1},v_{2})

\displaystyle=-2\operatorname{Im}\int_{C}\Tr\left((\dot{A}_{2}^{0,1})^{*}\wedge(\dot{\varphi}_{1})^{*}-(\dot{A}_{1}^{0,1})^{*}\wedge(\dot{\varphi}_{2})^{*}\right)=2\operatorname{Im}\int_{C}\Tr\left(\dot{\varphi}_{2}\wedge\dot{A}_{1}^{0,1}-\dot{\varphi}_{1}\wedge\dot{A}_{2}^{0,1}\right)

\displaystyle\omega_{K}(v_{1},v_{2})

\displaystyle=-2\operatorname{Im}\int_{C}\Tr\left((\dot{A}_{2}^{0,1})^{*}\wedge i(\dot{\varphi}_{1})^{*}-i(\dot{A}_{1}^{0,1})^{*}\wedge(\dot{\varphi}_{2})^{*}\right)=-2\operatorname{Re}\int_{C}\Tr\left(\dot{\varphi}_{2}\wedge\dot{A}_{1}^{0,1}-\dot{\varphi}_{1}\wedge\dot{A}_{2}^{0,1}\right)

(We derive these in Appendix
A
, and note here that the conventions are
{\mathrm{e}}^{i\theta}=1
,
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=1
.)
We note that
\Omega_{I}=\omega_{J}+i\omega_{K}
is simply

\displaystyle\Omega_{I}(v_{1},v_{2})

\displaystyle=-2i\int_{C}\Tr\left(\dot{\varphi}_{2}\wedge\dot{A}_{1}^{0,1}-\dot{\varphi}_{1}\wedge\dot{A}_{2}^{0,1}\right)

\displaystyle=2i\int_{C}\Tr\left(\dot{A}_{1}^{0,1}\wedge\dot{\varphi}_{2}-\dot{A}_{2}^{0,1}\wedge\dot{\varphi}_{1}\right).

1.3.
Why Affine Linearity?

This section is motivational and not at all rigorous. In this section, we’ll explain why one might expect that
[\omega_{I}]
is an affine linear function of
\alpha_{i}
, while
[\Omega_{I}]
is a linear function of
m_{i}
. (We are indebted to Max Zimet for this observation.)

In Duistermaat–Heckman
[
DH82
]
, one considers a symplectic manifold
(M,\omega)
with a (free) Hamiltonian torus action
T
, with associated
T
-equivariant moment map
\mu:M\to\mathfrak{t}^{*}
.
Given
\xi\in\mathfrak{t}^{*}
a regular value of
\mu
, define
Y_{\xi}=\mu^{-1}(\xi)
, and define
M_{\xi}=Y_{\xi}/T
. The principal
T
-bundle
\pi:Y_{\xi}\to M_{\xi}
is given by an element
c\in H^{2}(M_{\zeta},\Lambda)
where
\Lambda=\{X\in\mathfrak{t}:\exp X=\mathrm{id}\}
is the integral lattice of
T
.

Now, let
U
be an open neighborhood of some fixed
\xi_{0}
of regular values of
\mu
. There is a
T
-invariant Ehresmann connection on
\mu^{-1}(U)
, which gives us a
T
-equivariant map
\mu^{-1}(U)\to Y_{\xi_{0}}
by parallel transport over straight lines in
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
. Since
c(\xi)
is a continuous function of
\xi
valued in a discrete lattice, we have an honest identification of
H^{2}(M_{\xi},\Lambda_{\xi})
and
H^{2}(M_{\xi_{0}},\Lambda_{\xi_{0}})
. This induces an identification of
H^{2}(M_{\xi},\mathbb{R})
with
H^{2}(M_{\xi_{0}},\mathbb{R})
, which allows us to compare the symplectic forms
[\omega_{\xi}]\in H^{2}(M_{\xi},\mathbb{R}),[\omega_{\xi_{0}}]\in H^{2}(M_{\xi_{0}},\mathbb{R})
. Duistermaat–Heckman prove that
[\omega_{\xi}]-[\omega_{\xi_{0}}]
depends linearly on
\xi-\xi_{0}
.
Moreover, the constant coefficient of this linear map is an integer, namely
[\omega_{\xi}]-[\omega_{\xi_{0}}]=\langle c,\xi-\xi_{0}\rangle
, using the pairing between the two-form
c
valued in
\Lambda_{\xi_{0}}\subset\mathfrak{t}
and
\xi-\xi_{0}\in\mathfrak{t}^{*}
.

There is a well-known extension of this, in which a non-abelian compact Lie group
F
acting freely on
(M,\omega)

If an
F
-equivariant map
\mu:M\to\mathfrak{f}^{*}
exists
3
3

3

See HKLR p. 545 for a short discussion about obstructions and references.
,
\mu
is called a moment map, and is unique up to the addition of a constant element in
\mathfrak{f}^{*}
which is fixed by
F
, i.e. a character of
F
; we call this subspace
Z\subset\mathfrak{f}^{*}
. If
\xi
is in
Z
,
then
\mu^{-1}(\xi)/F
inherits a symplectic structure
4
4

4

If we take a point
\xi\in\mathfrak{f}^{*}
which is not fixed by
F
, but has isotropy subgroup
H
, then
\mu^{-1}(\xi)/H
has a symplectic structure.
.

There is a natural hyperKähler version of the above non-abelian statement, where
F
acts freely on a hyperKähler manifold
M
, preserving its hyperKähler structure. We now consider the triple of symplectic structures
(\omega_{I_{1}},\omega_{I_{2}},\omega_{I_{3}})
, and consequently, if it exists, the
F
-equivariant moment map
\mu=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3}):M\to\mathfrak{f}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Similarly one takes
\xi=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\in(Z\otimes\mathbb{R}^{3})^{\circ}
, where the superscript
\circ
denote the regular values of
\mu
, and considers
\mu^{-1}(\xi)/F=\cap_{i=1}^{3}\mu_{i}^{-1}(\xi_{i})/F
admitting a hyperKähler structure.

As an example of this,
[
Kro89a
]
, Kronheimer constructs ALE spaces associated to
\Gamma\subset SU(2)
as a finite hyperKähler quotient, and then quotes Duistermaat–Heckman to prove in
[
Kro89a
, Proposition 4.1i]
that the period map

\displaystyle(Z\otimes\mathbb{R}^{3})^{\circ}

\displaystyle\mapsto H^{2}(M_{\xi},\mathbb{R})^{\oplus 3}

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle\mapsto([\omega_{I_{1}}(\xi)],[\omega_{I_{2}}(\xi)],[\omega_{I_{3}}(\xi)])

is a linear isomorphism, and in particular that
[\omega_{I_{i}}(\xi)]
is a linear function of
\xi_{i}
alone.

Hitchin moduli spaces arise as hyperKähler quotients of an infinite-dimensional hyperKähler space
\mathcal{A}
by an infinite-dimensional gauge group
\mathcal{G}
. Consequently, Duistermaat–Heckman’s result does not apply.
However, it is still motivational. Morally, we want to say something along the lines of “if an analog of the Duistermaat–Heckman result held in this infinite-dimensional setting, it would imply the affine linearity that we prove in this paper.”
We refer the reader to Appendix
A
for further discussion of the hyperKähler moment maps: the complex moment map is proportional to
\overline{\partial}_{A}\varphi
while the real moment map is proportional to
F^{\perp}_{\overline{\partial}_{A}+{\partial}_{A}^{h}}+[\varphi,\varphi^{*_{h}}]
.
Ideally
\mathcal{A}
would be (1) slightly bigger than the space in Collier–Fredrickson–Wentworth so that it would be possible to vary
\bm{\alpha}
and (2) admit a hyperkähler structure (something it does not have in Collier–Fredrickson–Wentworth’s construction). However, we briefly review their configuration space which features a fixed framing near on the cylindrical ends each
p\in D
as motivation:

\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{\delta}\times\mathcal{D}_{\delta}

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\delta}

\displaystyle=d_{A_{0}}+L^{2}_{1,\delta}(\mathfrak{su}_{E}\otimes T^{*}C^{\times}),\qquad\qquad\qquad d_{A_{0}}=d+i\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{p}&\\
&1-\alpha_{p}\end{pmatrix}\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\delta}

\displaystyle=\{\varphi\in L^{2}_{-\delta}(\mathfrak{sl}_{E}\otimes K_{C})|\overline{\partial}_{A_{0}}\varphi\in L^{2}_{\delta}(\mathfrak{sl}_{E}\otimes K_{C}\otimes\overline{K}_{C})\},

while the framed gauge group
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{K}_{\delta,*}
defined in
[
CFW24
]
has Lie algebra is
\mathrm{Lie}(\mathcal{K}_{\delta,*})=L^{2}_{2,\delta}(\mathfrak{su}_{E})
. Notably, all elements are the identity at
p\in D
5
5

5

This is imposed by the
\mathcal{G}_{\delta,*}=\{\eta\in\mathcal{G}_{\delta}:\mathbf{b}(\eta)=\mathrm{I}\}
appearing above
[
CFW24
, (3.7)]
.
. Consequently,—being completely unrigorous—since elements of
\mathcal{G}
are the identity at
p\in D
, then it is possible for some distributional component supported along
D
to be fixed by
\mathcal{G}
.
Since
{\partial}_{{\overline{z}}}\frac{1}{z-p}=\pi\delta_{p}
, one can think of
\overline{\partial}_{A}\varphi
and
F_{\overline{\partial}_{A}+{\partial}_{A}^{h}}^{\perp}+[\varphi,\varphi^{*_{h}}]
as distribution-valued
2
-forms supported at
D
.
As is discussed further in Proposition
A.9
, the values of the complex and real moment maps are

\displaystyle M_{I}(\overline{\partial}_{A},\varphi)

\displaystyle=2i\overline{\partial}_{A}\varphi

\displaystyle=\underbrace{2i\sum_{p\in D}\begin{pmatrix}-m_{p}&n_{p}\\
0&m_{p}\end{pmatrix}\pi\delta_{p}d{\overline{z}}\wedge dz}_{\psi_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})}

\displaystyle\mu_{I}(\overline{\partial}_{A},\varphi)

\displaystyle=-(F^{\perp}_{D}+[\varphi,\varphi^{*}])

\displaystyle=\underbrace{-\sum_{p\in D}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{p}-\frac{1}{2}&\\
&\frac{1}{2}-\alpha_{p}\end{pmatrix}\pi\delta_{p}d{\overline{z}}\wedge dz}_{\psi_{\mathbb{R}}(\bm{\alpha})}.

In
[
CFW24
]
, the leaves of the framed extended moduli space
\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})
each have a hyperKähler structure which should come from their construction as
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})=\mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{-1}(\psi_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}))\cap\mu_{\mathbb{R}}^{-1}(\psi_{\mathbb{R}}(\bm{\alpha}))/\mathcal{G}
. Note that in
[
CFW24
]
, the moduli spaces for various values of
\mathbf{n}
can be identified since
\mathbf{n}
is an artifact of the framing.

1.4.
Torelli map for marked ALG-
D_{4}
spaces

In this section, we carefully state the Torelli Theorem of
[
CVZ24
]
. Despite being in the introduction, this section contains some new results specifying the image
\mathcal{P}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}\times\mathbb{C}^{4}
more explicitly (see Corollary
1.17
), since we will need this later.

Let
X
be an ALG-space of type
D_{4}
.
Based on
[
CC15
,
CC16
]
,
given
(X,g,I,J,K)
an ALG-
D_{4}
space, we may associate a
complex torus parameter

\tau\in\mathbb{H}/PSL(2,\mathbb{Z})
and
length scale

\ell
. Since
X\to\mathbb{C}
is elliptically fibered,
\ell
is defined by
\int_{F}\omega_{I}=\ell^{2}\mathrm{Im}\tau
for fiber
F
.

Let
I_{0}
denote the quadratic form on
\mathbb{Z}^{5}
given by the symmetric matrix

(1.7)

\begin{pmatrix}-2&1&1&1&1\\
1&-2&0&0&0\\
1&0&-2&0&0\\
1&0&0&-2&0\\
1&0&0&0&-2\end{pmatrix}.

Definition 1.7
.

An
adapted frame
of
H_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
is an isomorphism
f:\mathbb{Z}^{5}\to H_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
preserving quadratic forms and the fiber class:

(1)

f^{*}I=I_{0}
, and

(2)

f(2e_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}e_{i})=F\in H_{2}(\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m}),\mathbb{Z})
.

Note that since
2e_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}e_{i}
spans the kernel of
I_{0}
in
\mathbb{Z}^{5}
and
F
the kernel of
I
in
H_{2}(\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m}),\mathbb{Z})
, one necessarily has
f(2e_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}e_{i})=\pm F
for a frame satisfying the first condition only.

Definition 1.8
.

Given an adapted frame, let
S_{i}=f(e_{i})
for
i=0,\ldots,4
.
We call
\mathfrak{B}=(S_{0},\ldots,S_{4})

an admissible basis
of
H_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
.

We now define the relevant moduli space.

Definition 1.9
.

(1)

Fix
X
an ALG-
D_{4}
space with fixed
\tau\in\mathbb{H}
and scale
\ell>0
. A hyperkähler manifold
(M,g,I,J,K)
together with diffeomorphism
\mu:X\to M
is called a
marked ALG-
D_{4}
space
.

(2)

Two marked ALG-
D_{4}
spaces
(M,g,I,J,K)
and
(M^{\prime},g^{\prime},I^{\prime},J^{\prime},K^{\prime})
are considered
equivalent
if there exists a diffeomorphism
f:M\to M^{\prime}
such that
f^{*}g^{\prime}=g
,
f^{*}I^{\prime}=I
,
f^{*}J^{\prime}=J
,
f^{*}K^{\prime}=K
and

f_{*}\circ\mu_{*}=\mu^{\prime}_{*}:H_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})\to H_{2}(M^{\prime},\mathbb{Z}).

(3a)

Define
\mathcal{M}_{ALG}(X;D_{4},\tau,\ell)
as the set of equivalence classes of marked ALG-structures.

(3b)

Consider ALG-structures
(g,I,J,K)
on the fixed manifold
X
. Let
\mathcal{H}=H_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
and define
\Diff_{\mathcal{H}}(X)=\{f\in\Diff(X):f_{*}=\mathrm{Id}:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}\}
. Then set

\mathcal{M}_{ALG}(X;D_{4},\tau,\ell)=\{(g,I,J,K)\text{ ALG-structure on $X$}\}/\Diff_{\mathcal{H}}(X).

Both ways of defining the moduli space in (3a) and (3b) are equivalent (just pull the structure on
M
back to
X
by the marking diffeomorphism
\mu
).

The following Torelli theorem has been proven by
[
CVZ24
]
and the surjectivity part independently by
[
LL23
]
.

Theorem 1.10

(Torelli theorem for ALG spaces)
.

Let
X
be an ALG-
D_{4}
space with associated parameters
\tau,\ell
and let
\mathfrak{B}=(S_{0},\ldots,S_{4})
be an admissible basis of
H_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
.
The period domain of
\mathfrak{B}
is defined by

(1.8)

\mathcal{P}=(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{C})^{4}\setminus\bigcup\{((x_{j},z_{j})_{j=1}^{4}:\sum_{i=1}^{4}(\lambda_{0}-2\lambda_{i})(x_{i},z_{i})=\lambda_{0}(\ell^{2}\im\tau,0)\}

where the union is over integers
\lambda_{0},\ldots,\lambda_{4}
for which the class
\Sigma=\lambda_{0}S_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}\lambda_{i}S_{i}
has self-intersection
-2
.
Then the Torelli map

(1.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{B}}:\mathcal{M}_{ALG}(X,I_{0}^{*},\tau,\ell)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle[(g,I,J,K)]

\displaystyle\longmapsto\left\{\left(\int_{S_{i}}\omega_{I},\int_{S_{i}}\Omega_{I}\right)\right\}_{i=1}^{4}

is a bijection.

Remark 1.11

(Explanation of
\mathcal{P}
)
.

Observe that the class
[F]
of the elliptic (Hitchin) fiber
F
satisfies

(1.10)

\int_{F}\omega_{I}=\ell^{2}\im\tau,\qquad\int_{F}\Omega_{I}=0,\ \ i=1,2,3.

Indeed, each fiber is a ‘BAA brane’, namely complex for the complex structure
I
and Lagrangian with respect to
J
and
K
. Since

2[S_{0}]+[S_{1}]+[S_{2}]+[S_{3}]+[S_{4}]=[F]
, the corresponding combination of the basis periods must also vanish.

However, this is not the only constraint. Recall also that an integral homology class
[\Sigma]
of self-intersection
-2
in an ALG space can be represented by a curve
which is holomorphic with respect to some complex structure
aI+bJ+cK
,
a^{2}+b^{2}+c^{2}=1
, hence these
classes represent further constraints. For example, this implies that the set of periods of such a curve cannot all vanish, see
[
LL23
]
. To understand the
further constraints, let us first write
\Sigma=\sum_{i=0}^{4}\lambda_{i}S_{i}
. We then compute that

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}\omega_{I}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{5}\lambda_{i}\int_{S_{i}}\omega_{I}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{0}}{2}\left(\int_{F}\omega_{I}-\sum_{i=1}^{4}\int_{S_{i}}\omega_{I}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{4}\lambda_{i}\int_{S_{i}}\omega_{I}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{0}}{2}\int_{F}\omega_{I}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}\left(\lambda_{i}-\frac{\lambda_{0}}{2}\right)\int_{S_{i}}\omega_{I},

with a similar computation for the integrals of
\Omega_{I}
. We conclude from this that

\sum_{i=1}^{4}(\lambda_{0}-2\lambda_{i})\left(\int_{S_{i}}\omega_{I},\int_{S_{i}}\Omega_{I}\right)\neq\lambda_{0}(\ell^{2}\im\tau,0)

This is precisely the definition of
\mathcal{P}
in (
1.8
) appearing above.

Remark 1.12
.

We have stated the Torelli theorem only for ALG-spaces of type
I_{0}^{*}
since this is the only asymptotic geometry we will encounter in this article. The Torelli theorem is more generally true for ALG-structures if the order is at least 2. In the
I_{0}^{*}
-case this places no further restriction, see
[
CVZ24
]

As written, it appears that
\mathcal{P}
is defined relative to a particular choice of basis
\mathfrak{B}
. We will see that in fact this domain is
independent of that basis.
Let

\OO(\mathbb{Z}^{5},I_{0})^{+}=\left\{A\in\OO(\mathbb{Z}^{5},I_{0}):A(2e_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}e_{i})=2e_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}e_{i}\right\}.

There is a natural right action of
\OO(\mathbb{Z}^{5},I_{0})^{+}
on adapted frames, namely
f\cdot A=f\circ A
for
A\in\OO(\mathbb{Z}^{5},I_{0})^{+}
. If
A=(a_{ij})\in\OO(\mathbb{Z}^{5},I_{0})^{+}
then the corresponding right action on admissible bases is given by
\mathfrak{B}\cdot A=A^{t}\cdot\mathfrak{B}
where we think of
\mathfrak{B}
as the column vector
(S_{0},\ldots,S_{4})^{t}
, more precisely

S_{j}^{\prime}=f^{\prime}(e_{j})=f(A(e_{j}))=\sum_{i=0}^{4}a_{ij}f(e_{i})=\sum_{i=0}^{4}a_{ij}S_{i}.

Lemma 1.13
.

The period map satisfies the equivariance condition

\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{B}\cdot A}([(g,I,J,K)])=\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{B}}([(g,I,J,K)])\cdot A

for
A\in\OO(\mathbb{Z}^{5},I_{0})^{+}
.

Proof.

By linearity of
C\mapsto\int_{C}\omega_{I}
(resp.
C\mapsto\int_{C}\Omega_{I}
)

\int_{S_{j}^{\prime}}\omega_{I}=\sum_{i=0}^{4}a_{ij}\int_{S_{i}}\omega_{I}\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{S_{j}^{\prime}}\Omega_{I}=\sum_{i=0}^{4}a_{ij}\int_{S_{i}}\Omega_{I}.

If we set
x=(x_{0},\ldots,x_{4})^{t}
for
x_{i}=\int_{S_{i}}\omega_{I}
and
z=(z_{0},\ldots,z_{4})^{t}
for
z_{i}=\int_{S_{i}}\Omega_{I}
and
x^{\prime},z^{\prime}
correspondingly this amounts to

x^{\prime}=A^{t}\cdot x\quad\text{and}\quad z^{\prime}=A^{t}\cdot z.

Using the homological relation
2S_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}S_{i}=F
together with
\int_{F}\omega_{I}=4\pi^{2}
and
\int_{F}\Omega_{I}=0
we may express
x_{0}=2\pi^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{4}x_{i}
and
z_{0}=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{4}z_{i}
. Hence, we obtain

\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}^{\prime}\\
x_{2}^{\prime}\\
x_{3}^{\prime}\\
x_{4}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=\hat{A}^{t}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}\\
x_{2}\\
x_{3}\\
x_{4}\end{pmatrix}+\hat{b}\quad\text{and}\quad\begin{pmatrix}z_{1}^{\prime}\\
z_{2}^{\prime}\\
z_{3}^{\prime}\\
z_{4}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=\hat{A}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}z_{1}\\
z_{2}\\
z_{3}\\
z_{4}\end{pmatrix}

for
\hat{A}\in\GL(4,\mathbb{R})
and
\hat{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
defined by
\hat{a}_{ij}=a_{ij}-\frac{1}{2}a_{0j}
and
\hat{b}_{j}=2\pi^{2}a_{0j}
,
i,j\in\{1,2,3,4\}
. This determines a right action of
\OO(\mathbb{Z}^{5},I_{0})^{+}
on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
by affine-linear and on
\mathbb{C}^{4}
by linear transformations which we will denote by
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})\mapsto(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})\cdot A
. The following is then clear.
∎

We shall convince ourselves in the following that the right action of
\OO(\mathbb{Z}^{5},I_{0})^{+}
on the target
\mathbb{R}^{4}\times\mathbb{C}^{4}
restricts to an action on the period domain and that the period domain does not depend on the choice of admissible basis
\mathfrak{B}
.

Lemma 1.14
.

The period domain
\mathcal{P}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}\times\mathbb{C}^{4}
is invariant under the action of
\OO(\mathbb{Z}^{5},I_{0})^{+}
. More precisely, if
\mathfrak{B}=(S_{0},\ldots,S_{4})
is an admissible basis, then
\mathcal{H}_{\bm{\lambda}}\cdot A=\mathcal{H}_{A^{-1}\bm{\lambda}}
for
A\in\OO(\mathbb{Z}^{5},I_{0})^{+}
,
\bm{\lambda}=(\lambda_{0},\ldots,\lambda_{4})\in\mathbb{Z}^{5}
and the codimension-three subspaces

\mathcal{H}_{\bm{\lambda}}=\bigl\{\sum_{i=1}^{4}(\lambda_{0}-2\lambda_{i})(x_{i},z_{i})=\lambda_{0}(4\pi^{2},0)\bigr\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}\times\mathbb{C}^{4}

where
[\Sigma]=\sum_{i=0}^{4}\lambda_{i}[S_{i}]
has self-intersection
-2
.

Proof.

First recall that after setting
x_{0}=\frac{1}{2}(4\pi^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{4}x_{i})
and
z_{0}=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{4}z_{i}
the inhomogeneous equation defining
\mathcal{H}_{\bm{\lambda}}
is equivalent to the homogeneous equation
\sum_{i=0}^{4}\lambda_{i}(x_{i},z_{i})=(0,0)
. If
[\Sigma]=\sum_{i=0}^{4}\lambda_{i}[S_{i}]
and
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}=A^{t}\cdot\mathfrak{B}
for
A\in\OO(\mathbb{Z}^{5},I_{0})^{+}
, then

\Sigma=\bm{\lambda}^{t}\cdot\mathfrak{B}=(A^{-1}\bm{\lambda})^{t}\cdot A^{t}\mathfrak{B}=(\bm{\lambda}^{\prime})^{t}\cdot\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}=\sum_{i=0}^{4}\lambda_{i}^{\prime}S_{i}^{\prime}

with
\bm{\lambda}^{\prime}=A^{-1}\bm{\lambda}
for
\bm{\lambda}=(\lambda_{0},\ldots,\lambda_{4})
and
\bm{\lambda}^{\prime}=(\lambda_{0}^{\prime},\ldots,\lambda_{4}^{\prime})
.

Now for
\mathbf{x}=(x_{0},\ldots,x_{4})
and
\mathbf{z}=(z_{0},\ldots,z_{4})

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{4}\lambda_{i}(x_{i},z_{i})=(0,0)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\bm{\lambda}^{t}\cdot\mathbf{x}=\bm{\lambda}^{t}\cdot\mathbf{z}=0

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow(A^{-1}\bm{\lambda})^{t}\cdot A^{t}\mathbf{x}=(A^{-1}\bm{\lambda})^{t}\cdot A^{t}\mathbf{z}=0

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow(\bm{\lambda}^{\prime})^{t}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=(\bm{\lambda}^{\prime})^{t}\cdot\mathbf{z}^{\prime}=0\Longleftrightarrow\sum_{i=0}^{4}\lambda_{i}^{\prime}(x_{i}^{\prime},z_{i}^{\prime})=(0,0)

for
\bm{\lambda}^{\prime}=A^{-1}\bm{\lambda}
as above,
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=A^{t}\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}=A^{t}\mathbf{z}
.
This equivalence show that
\mathcal{H}_{\bm{\lambda}}\cdot A=\mathcal{H}_{A^{-1}\bm{\lambda}}
for
A\in\OO(\mathbb{Z}^{5},I_{0})^{+}
.
∎

Corollary 1.15
.

The period domain
\mathcal{P}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}\times\mathbb{C}^{4}
does not depend on the choice of admissible basis
\mathfrak{B}=(S_{0},\ldots,S_{4})
of
H_{2}(\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m}),\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof.

The orthogonal group
\OO(\mathbb{Z}^{5},I_{0})^{+}
acts transitively on the set of admissible bases. Now
\bm{\lambda}\in\mathbb{Z}^{5}
gives rise to a codimension-three subspace
\mathcal{H}_{\bm{\lambda}}
with respect to the basis
\mathfrak{B}=(S_{0},\ldots,S_{4})
if and only if
C=\sum_{i=0}^{4}\lambda_{i}S_{i}
has self-intersection
-2
. Reexpressing
C=\sum_{i=0}^{4}\lambda_{i}^{\prime}S_{i}^{\prime}
for
\bm{\lambda}^{\prime}=A^{-1}\bm{\lambda}
and
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}=A^{t}\cdot\mathfrak{B}
for
A\in\OO(\mathbb{Z}^{5},I_{0})^{+}
as above yields that
\bm{\lambda}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{5}
gives rise to a codimension-three subspace
\mathcal{H}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\prime}}
with respect to the basis
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}=(S_{0}^{\prime},\ldots,S_{4}^{\prime})
. But
\mathcal{H}_{\bm{\lambda}^{\prime}}
is the translate of
\mathcal{H}_{\bm{\lambda}}
under the element
A\in\OO(\mathbb{Z}^{5},I_{0})^{+}
which proves the claim.
∎

It remains to determine which linear combinations of the
S_{i}
have self-intersection
-2
.

Lemma 1.16
.

The classes
[\Sigma]=\lambda_{0}[S_{0}]+\sum_{i=1}^{4}\lambda_{i}[S_{i}]
of self-intersection
-2
in
H_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
have
coefficients
(\lambda_{0},\bm{\lambda})\in\mathbb{Z}^{5}
given by

(1)

\bm{\lambda}=k(1,1,1,1)
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\lambda_{0}=2k\pm 1
,

(2)

\bm{\lambda}=k(1,1,1,1)\pm e_{i}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
i\in\{1,\ldots,4\}
and
\lambda_{0}=2k,2k\pm 1
,

(3)

\bm{\lambda}=k(1,1,1,1)\pm(e_{i}+e_{j})
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
i\neq j\in\{1,\ldots,4\}
and
\lambda_{0}=2k\pm 1
,

where the
e_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,4
are the standard basis vectors of
\mathbb{R}^{4}
and
\bm{\lambda}=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{4})
.

Proof.

Using the intersection matrix (
2.3
),

\displaystyle[\Sigma]\cdot[\Sigma]=-2\Bigl(\lambda_{0}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}\lambda_{i}^{2}-\lambda_{0}\sum_{i=1}^{4}\lambda_{i}\Bigr)=-2

hence

\lambda_{0}^{2}-\lambda_{0}\bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{4}\lambda_{i}\bigr)+\bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{4}\lambda_{i}^{2})-1=0

This quadratic equation has discriminant,

D=\frac{1}{4}\bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{4}\lambda_{i}\bigr)^{2}+1-\sum_{i=1}^{4}\lambda_{i}=1-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i<j}(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j})^{2}\leq 1,

so there can be integer solutions only when
D\in\{0,\frac{1}{4},1\}
. The solutions (1), (2) and (3) correspond to these three cases.
∎

Corollary 1.17
.

The period domain
\mathcal{P}
is the complement of the collection of codimension-three planes

\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{H}_{k}\cup\mathcal{H}_{k,i_{0}}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{k,i_{0}}\cup\mathcal{H}_{k,i_{1},i_{2}},

where

(1)

\mathcal{H}_{k}=\{\sum_{i=1}^{4}x_{i}=(2k+1)\ell^{2}\im\tau,\,\sum_{i=1}^{4}z_{i}=0\}
,

(2)

\mathcal{H}_{k,i_{0}}=\{x_{i_{0}}=k\ell^{2}\im\tau,\,z_{i_{0}}=0\}
,

\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{k,i_{0}}=\{2x_{i_{0}}-\sum_{i=1}^{4}x_{i}=(2k+1)\ell^{2}\im\tau,\,2z_{i_{0}}-\sum_{i=1}^{4}z_{i}=0\}
,
i_{0}\in\{1,\ldots,4\}
,

(3)

\mathcal{H}_{k,i_{1},i_{2}}=\{2(x_{i_{1}}+x_{i_{2}})-\sum_{i=1}^{4}x_{i})=(2k+1)\ell^{2}\im\tau,\,2(z_{i_{1}}+z_{i_{2}})-\sum_{i=1}^{4}z_{i}=0\}
,

i_{1}\neq i_{2}\in\{1,\ldots,4\}
.

Example 1.18
.

The spheres
S_{0},S_{1},S_{2},S_{3},S_{4}
themselves have self-intersection
-2
in
H_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
. The corresponding codimension-three planes are then

(1)

\mathcal{H}_{S_{0}}=\{\sum_{i=1}^{4}x_{i}=\ell^{2}\im\tau,\,\sum_{i=1}^{4}z_{i}=0\}
,

(2)

\mathcal{H}_{S_{i}}=\{x_{i}=0,\,z_{i}=0\}
,
i=1,\ldots,4
.

Note that the intersections of these codimension three planes with
\mathbb{R}^{4}\times\{0\}
bound
the 4-simplex with vertices
V_{0}=(0,0,0,0)
and
V_{i}=\ell^{2}\im\tau e_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,4
.

1.5.
Organization of the Paper

§
2

A key tool in this paper is the fact that the various moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
fit together into a larger ‘extended’ moduli space, in which the complex masses (and the real masses) vary. The larger moduli space
\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})
constructed in
[
CFW24
]
in which the complex masses vary is particularly useful for us, and we discuss the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action on the space.

§
3

We review the chamber structure for the space of real mass parameters
\bm{\alpha}
. This leads to an explicit description of the nilpotent cone for
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{0})
, which in turn gives a distinguished
2
-homology basis for
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
.

§
4

The first main theorem appears in Theorem
4.1
. It is a statement about the integral of
\omega_{I}
and
\Omega_{J}
over the distinguished
2
-homology basis for
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
. Notably, as we mentioned before, each integral of
\omega_{I}
is an affine linear function depending on
\bm{\alpha}
while
\Omega
is a linear function depending on
\mathbf{m}
.

§
5

In this section, we compute the integral of
\omega_{I}
over the distinguished
2
-homology basis for
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{0})
. We then use the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action on the larger moduli space
\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})
to compute the integral of
\omega_{I}
over the distinguished
2
-homology basis of
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
. The main technical result here is Proposition
5.9
, which implies Proposition
5.12
.

§
6

In this section, we prove in Proposition
6.5
we prove that the tautological
1
-form
\tau
is a primitive for the holomorphic symplectic form
\Omega_{I}
on the complement of a divisor, that we call the polar divisor (see Definition
6.8
); lastly, in Corollary
6.16
, we prove that the integral of
\Omega_{I}
over
S^{2}
can be computed via from the intersection numbers of
S^{2}
with the spheres
\Sigma_{j}=\sigma_{j}^{+}-\sigma_{j}^{-}
formed from the polar sections.

§
7

The goal of this section is to parameterize an open dense subset of the Higgs bundle moduli space, as well as the tautological
1
-form, so that the required intersection numbers can be computed.

§
8

In this section, we compute the integral of
\Omega_{I}
over the distinguished
2
-homology basis for
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
when
\bm{\alpha}
is in two (of the 24) chambers.

§
9

Finally, we discuss the action of the affine
D_{4}
Coxeter group on various spaces and prove that the Torelli map is equivariant with respect to this action. Using this, we prove the
near
surjectivity of the Torelli map onto the period domain in Theorem
9.2
.

Lastly, we note that in Appendix
A
, we write out the
\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times U(1)
family of hyperKähler structures one could naturally put on the moduli space of Higgs bundles,
while in Appendix
B
we discuss the location of the homology spheres in the weakly parabolic (i.e.
\mathbf{m}\neq\mathbf{0}
) setting.

H_{2}(\mathcal{M},\mathbb{Z})

U(1)

2.
Extended Moduli Space

A key tool in this paper is the fact that the various moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
fit together into a larger ‘extended’ moduli space
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}

by letting the real and complex masses vary. In other words,
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
is a fibration over an appropriate open dense subset in
(0,\frac{1}{2})^{4}\times\mathbb{C}^{4}

with fiber at
(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
equal to
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
. This construction has been achieved in different categories by various authors.
First, regarding the individual fibers, Yokogawa
[
Yok93
]
constructed the moduli space of semistable parabolic Higgs bundles
for a given
(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
as a quasi-projective variety. Analytic constructions of the parabolic moduli spaces have also been given, first
by Konno
[
Kon93
]
in the strongly parabolic rank
2
case, i.e., where
\mathbf{m}=0
, then by Nakajima
[
Nak96
]
in the weakly
parabolic (
\mathbf{m}\neq 0
) rank
2
case. More recently, the first author, together with Collier and Wentworth
[
CFW24
]

constructed
\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})
6
6

6

They use the notation
\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})
for this space, while they use
\mathcal{P}(\bm{\alpha})\to\mathbb{C}
for the space of parabolic logarithmic
\lambda
-connections.
, which as a set is
\sqcup_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{C}^{4}}\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
. This analytic construction gives rise to a hyperKähler metric on the smooth locus of each
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
. We
review the construction of
[
CFW24
]
later in this section with a particular emphasis on the hyperKähler structure.

In any of these constructions,
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
acquires the structure of a complex manifold for generic parameters
(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
. Here genericity
of
(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})\in(0,\frac{1}{2})^{4}\times\mathbb{C}^{4}
corresponds to the condition that
\bm{\alpha}
-semistability implies
\bm{\alpha}
-stability (see Definition
2.1
below).

In this section we describe this fibration and some of its basic properties.

2.1.
Genericity and Nakajima’s Walls

Following Nakajima in
[
Nak96
]
, we define the subset of generic parameters
(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
as follows.

Definition 2.1
.

(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})\in(0,\frac{1}{2})^{4}\times\mathbb{C}^{4}
is called
generic
if
\bm{\alpha}
-semistability implies
\bm{\alpha}
-stability.

This is the complement of a finite union of codimension-three planes.

Proposition 2.2
.

The set
\mathcal{R}
of generic parameters
(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})\in(0,\frac{1}{2})^{4}\times\mathbb{C}^{4}
is characterized as

(2.1)

\mathcal{R}=(0,\textstyle\frac{1}{2})^{4}\times\mathbb{C}^{4}\setminus\bigcup_{d\in\mathbb{Z},\mathbf{e}\in\{0,1\}^{4}}\mathcal{H}_{d,\mathbf{e}},

where

(2.2)

\mathcal{H}_{d,\mathbf{e}}=\{(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})\in(0,\textstyle\frac{1}{2})^{4}\times\mathbb{C}^{4}:d+\sum_{i=1}^{4}(e_{i}+(-1)^{e_{i}}\alpha_{i})=0\ \ \&\ \ \sum_{i=1}^{4}(-1)^{e_{i}}m_{i}=0.\}.

This is
[
Nak96
, Lemma 2.4]
, adapted to our setting.

Corollary 2.3
.

If
(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{0})
is generic, then
(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
is generic for all
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{C}^{4}
.

This Corollary suggests that although genericity has been defined for pairs
(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
, it makes sense to refer to a given
\bm{\alpha}
as being generic.
We note, finally, that since
\mathcal{R}
is the complement of a collection of codimension-three planes,

Corollary 2.4
.

\mathcal{R}
is both connected and simply-connected.

2.2.
Smoothness of the fibration

We now review the smooth bundle structure of
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
, with the added feature that the fibers are quasiprojective varieties.

Proposition 2.5

(Corollary of
[
ISS19a
,
ISS19b
]
)
.

The complex manifolds
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
, fit together to form a smooth fiber bundle

\pi:\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}=\coprod_{(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})\in\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})\to\mathcal{R}
. Each fiber
\pi^{-1}((\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m}))=\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})

is a smooth affine variety with completion a rational elliptic surface. The compactifying divisor is an
I_{0}^{*}
fiber.

Proof.

Given a contractible open set
U\subset\mathcal{R}
containing the base point
(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
, we must produce a local trivialization
\mathcal{X}|_{U}\cong U\times\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha_{0}},\mathbf{m_{0}})
. For the 4-punctured sphere, such a trivialization exists by the work of Ivanics, Stipsicz and
Szabó
[
ISS19a
]
,
[
ISS19b
]
who construct the weakly parabolic moduli spaces in that case as follows.

For any set of complex masses
\mathbf{m}=(m_{1},m_{2},m_{3},m_{4})\in\mathbb{C}^{4}
, we consider the family of spectral curves
\Sigma_{b}\subset K_{C}(D)

parameterized by
b\in\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{m})
, the Hitchin base as in Proposition
2.13
. By construction, this family depends
smoothly on
\mathbf{m}
, but is independent of
\bm{\alpha}
. It extends to a pencil of curves in the projective compactification of
K_{C}(D)
which, since

K_{C}(D)=\mathcal{O}(2)
, equals the Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{2}=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{O}(2)\oplus\mathcal{O})
. The compactifying divisor curve
C_{\infty}
has
five components: the section at infinity
\sigma_{\infty}=\mathbb{F}_{2}\setminus K_{C}(D)
and the four projective lines
F_{1},F_{2},F_{3},F_{4}
,
which are the fibers of
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}\in C
in the ruling
\mathbb{F}_{2}\to C
. The base locus of this pencil is
F_{1}\cup F_{2}\cup F_{3}\cup F_{4}

since all spectral curves pass through the points
\lim_{z\to p_{i}}\frac{\pm m_{i}}{z-p_{i}}dz
, regarded as points in the total space of
K_{C}(D)
.
In particular there are two base points in
F_{i}
if
m_{i}\neq 0
, and otherwise there is a single one with multiplicity 2. This results in 8 base points,
and blowing up all of these results in a surface
X
biholomorphic to the rational elliptic surface
\mathbb{CP}^{2}\#9\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}
. Under the Hitchin
fibration, the pencil becomes an elliptic fibration on
X
, and
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
is identified with
\mathcal{M}=X\setminus C_{\infty}
. (Here
C_{\infty}

is identified with its proper transform in
X
). This show that the moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
are mutually diffeomorphic and form a smooth family
over
\mathcal{R}
.

Since the fiber
C_{\infty}
is a singular fiber of type
I_{0}^{*}
, this argument implies that the weakly parabolic moduli spaces compactify to a rational elliptic surface
with
I_{0}^{*}
-fiber at infinity.
∎

Remark 2.6
.

To foreshadow a later discussion, we observe that by a result of Hein
[
Hei12
]
, each open fiber admits a complete hyperKähler metric of type ALG-
D4
.
This fiber also admits Hitchin’s hyperKähler metric, and it is not a priori clear that these two metrics coincide. Failure to do so would be very surprising, and
could only happen if the Hitchin metric didn’t have finite energy, i.e. the Riemannian curvature tensor was not
L^{2}
-integrable. We proved in
[
FMSW22
]
that when
\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{0}
, Hitchin’s metric
is ALG. In the sequel to this paper we prove that this remains true even for nonzero complex masses.

Remark 2.7
.

The construction in Proposition
2.5
is equivalent to a familiar description of the strongly parabolic moduli space
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{0})
, which has
been called the ‘toy model’
[
Hau98
]
. That description proceeds by taking the product
\mathbb{C}\times T^{2}_{\tau}
for some
\tau\in\mathbb{H}
,
and dividing by the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
action which acts by
\pm 1
in each factor. The quotient
(\mathbb{C}\times T^{2}_{\tau})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
has 4 orbifold singularities, all
lying in the central fiber
(\{0\}\times T^{2}_{\tau})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, which is thus the pillow-case orbifold. Blowing up these 4 orbifold singularities yields the elliptic
surface
\mathcal{M}
with a singular fiber of type
I_{0}^{*}
over
0\in\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. This fiber is identified with the nilpotent cone of the corresponding strongly
parabolic Higgs bundle moduli space and consists of 5 spheres
S_{0},S_{1},S_{2},S_{3},S_{4}
which constitute a basis for
H_{2}(\mathcal{M},\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{5}

for which the intersection form agrees with the intersection form for the affine
D_{4}
Dynkin diagram:

(2.3)

\begin{pmatrix}-2&1&1&1&1\\
1&-2&0&0&0\\
1&0&-2&0&0\\
1&0&0&-2&0\\
1&0&0&0&-2\end{pmatrix}

This space can be compactified by adding another
I_{0}^{*}
fiber at infinity, and the resulting space is a rational elliptic surface. The modulus
\tau\in\mathbb{H}

of the torus fiber is determined by the cross-ratio of the points
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}\in D
, see
[
FMSW22
]
.

2.3.
Second Homology Bundle

At each point of
\mathcal{R}
, the homology lattice
H_{2}(\mathcal{M},\mathbb{Z})
has rank
5
, and has an integral basis
S_{0},S_{1},S_{2},S_{3},S_{4}

where the intersection form agrees with the intersection form for the affine
D_{4}
Dynkin diagram. We call such bases
admissible
.
When
\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{0}
and
\bm{\alpha}

lies in a certain subset of
\mathcal{R}
, there is a particularly natural basis where the central sphere
S_{0}
corresponds to the set of stable
parabolic bundles with Higgs field
\varphi\equiv 0
, and the other
S_{i}
can also be explicitly described in terms of Higgs data, see Section
3
below.
An important point is that for any integral basis with this intersection form, the cycle
2S_{0}+S_{1}+S_{2}+S_{3}+S_{4}
is homologous
to the Hitchin fiber
F
, and hence vanishes in homology relative to the fiber at infinity.

It is not
a priori
clear how to choose a basis for
H_{2}
as
\mathbf{m}
varies, but in fact, we may propagate this natural basis to all of
\mathcal{R}
.
Proposition
2.5
implies:

Proposition 2.8
.

The collection of homology lattices

\mathcal{H}=\coprod_{(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})\in\mathcal{R}}H_{2}(\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m}),\mathbb{Z})

forms a local system of abelian groups over
\mathcal{R}
. In fact, this local system is trivial, so a choice of basis at

(\bm{\alpha_{0}},\mathbf{0})
(or indeed any
(\bm{\alpha_{0}},\mathbf{m_{0}})
in
\mathcal{R}
) may be extended
by parallel transport over all of
\mathcal{R}
.

Proof.

Over any contractible open set
U\ni(\bm{\alpha_{0}},\mathbf{m_{0}})
, the local trivialization
\mathcal{X}|_{U}\cong U\times\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha_{0}},\mathbf{m_{0}})
induces a canonical isomorphism

H_{2}(\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m}),\mathbb{Z})\cong H_{2}(\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha_{0}},\mathbf{m_{0}}),\mathbb{Z})\quad\forall\,(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})\in U,

which makes
\mathcal{H}
a local system of abelian groups.

Tensoring
\mathcal{H}
with
{\mathcal{R}}
yields a vector bundle which has a canonical flat Gauss–Manin connection. Since
\mathcal{R}
is connected and simply-connected,
we can extend any basis
\mathfrak{B}_{0}
of
H_{2}(\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha_{0}},\mathbf{m_{0}}),\mathbb{Z})
at a given base point

(\bm{\alpha_{0}},\mathbf{m_{0}})\in\mathcal{R}
to a parallel basis
\mathfrak{B}
of
\mathcal{H}
over all of
\mathcal{R}
.
∎

2.4.
Hitchin Torelli Map

We are now in a position to define one of the main topics of this paper.

Definition 2.9

(
\mathfrak{B}
-Torelli map)
.

Fix a parallel admissible basis
\mathfrak{B}=(S_{0},\ldots,S_{4})
of the local system of homology lattices
\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{R}
in Proposition
2.8
. We then define the
\mathfrak{B}
-
Torelli map
by

\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{B}}:\mathcal{R}\longrightarrow(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{C})^{4},\quad(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})\longmapsto\left\{\left(\int_{S_{i}}\omega_{I},\int_{S_{i}}\Omega_{I}\right)\right\}_{i=1}^{4}.

Each of these integrals is called a
period
of the hyperKähler structure of
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
.

2.5.
Extended Moduli Space of Collier–Fredrickson–Wentworth

We need a bit more information about the smooth fibration
\pi:\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathcal{R}
. For each
(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})\in\mathcal{R}
, there is
a distinguished complex structure
I
and holomorphic symplectic form
\Omega_{I}
on
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
. This complex structure is
characterized as the one for which this moduli space is an algebraic completely integrable system.
We seek to understand how
(I,\Omega_{I})
behaves as we vary
\mathbf{m}
.

Fixing a generic
\bm{\alpha}
, let us restrict the fibration
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
to a fibration
\pi_{\bm{\alpha}}:\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})\to\{\bm{\alpha}\}\times\mathbb{C}^{4}
. This restricted
total space
\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})
was constructed by Collier, Fredrickson and Wentworth
[
CFW24
]
.
As a smooth fiber bundle,
\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})
is simply the union of fibers of
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
over
\{\bm{\alpha}\}\times\mathbb{C}^{4}
, but this space has a richer structure
as a smooth quasi-projective variety. To exhibit this, we describe how it is constructed analytically.

Figure 2.1.

Let
C
be a compact curve and
D=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{N}\}
a divisor in
C
. For simplicity, we restrict to rank
2
Higgs bundles, and of course are primarily
concerned with the case where
C=\mathbb{CP}^{1}
and
N=4
. Endow the open Riemann surface
C^{\times}=C\setminus D
with a conformally compatible
metric
g
for which in a neighborhood of each
p_{j}
,
g
takes the form
d\tau^{2}+d\theta^{2}
, where
z
is a local holomorphic coordinate in
a punctured disk
\Delta^{\times}(p_{i})
,
z=e^{w}
and
w=-\tau+i\theta
.
Fix a hermitian metric
h_{0}
on
E|_{C^{\times}}
and a unitary frame
(e_{1},e_{2})
over each
\Delta^{\times}(p_{i})
. Let further
A_{0}
be a fixed unitary
connection on
E|_{C^{\times}}
of the form

d_{A_{0}}=d+\begin{pmatrix}\alpha^{(1)}(p_{i})&0\\
0&\alpha^{(2)}(p_{i})\end{pmatrix}d\theta

on each punctured disk.
We use exponentially weighted Sobolev spaces
L^{2}_{k,\delta}(E)
with norm

\|u\|_{L^{2}_{k,\delta}}=\Bigl(\int_{C^{\times}}e^{\tau\delta}\,\Bigl(\sum_{i=0}^{k}|\nabla_{0}^{(i)}u|^{2}\Bigr)\,dA_{g}\Bigr)^{1/2},\ \ \delta\in\mathbb{R}.

This norm can be used, with obvious modifications, for sections of the various bundles below.

Now consider the space of
L^{2}_{k,\delta}
connections

\mathcal{A}_{\delta}=d_{A_{0}}+L^{2}_{1,\delta}(T^{*}C\otimes\mathfrak{su}(E))

and Higgs fields

\mathcal{D}_{\delta}=\{\varphi\in L^{2}_{-\delta}(T^{*}C^{1,0}\otimes\mathfrak{sl}(E)):\bar{\partial}_{A_{0}}\varphi\in L^{2}_{\delta}\}.

Clearly
L^{2}_{1,\delta}(T^{*}C^{1,0}\otimes\mathfrak{sl}(E))\subset\mathcal{D}_{\delta}
and by
[
CFW24
]
[Proposition 3.8],
this subspace is the kernel of the residue map

\Res=r:\mathcal{D}_{\delta}\to\mathbb{C}^{N},\quad\varphi\mapsto\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\varphi(\tau,\theta)=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{N})

where
\varphi=\varphi(z)\frac{dz}{z}=\varphi(w)dw
, and we identify the matrix

\begin{pmatrix}-m_{i}&0\\
0&m_{i}\end{pmatrix}
with the complex number
m_{i}
.
Hence,
\mathcal{D}_{\delta}
is a finite-dimensional
extension of
L^{2}_{1,\delta}(T^{*}C^{1,0}\otimes\mathfrak{sl}(E))
.

Now write

\mathcal{B}_{\delta}=\{(A,\varphi)\in\mathcal{A}_{\delta}\times\mathcal{D}_{\delta}:\bar{\partial}_{A}\varphi=0\};

this is the space of parabolic Higgs pairs, and we let
\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(\bm{\alpha})
be the subset of
\bm{\alpha}
-stable ones.
The complex gauge group
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}
acts on
\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(\bm{\alpha})
with quotient

\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})=\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(\bm{\alpha})/\mathcal{G}_{\delta}

the (extended) moduli space of parabolic Higgs bundles. This is independent of
\delta
for
\delta>0
sufficiently small.

By equivariance, the residue map descends to a map
\pi_{\bm{\alpha}}:\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})\to\mathbb{C}^{N}
, and we define the space of parabolic Higgs bundles with fixed
complex masses as
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})=\pi_{\bm{\alpha}}^{-1}(\mathbf{m})
.
In particular,
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{0})
is the moduli space of strongly parabolic Higgs bundles.

The following is proved in
[
CFW24
]
:

Theorem 2.10

(
[
CFW24
]
)
.

(1)

\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})
is a complex manifold of complex dimension
6g-6+3N
;

(2)

\pi_{\bm{\alpha}}
is a holomorphic submersion;

(3)

the fibers
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
are hyperKähler manifolds of complex dimension
6g-6+2N
.

This result generalizes earlier work of Konno
[
Kon93
]
and Nakajima
[
Nak96
]
.

In our setting,
g=0
and
N=4
, so
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})=6
and
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})=2
.

Remark 2.11
.

It does not seem to be known in general if the projection
\pi_{\bm{\alpha}}:\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{N}
is a smooth fiber bundle. However, as already pointed
out in Proposition
2.5
, this is transparent for the four-punctured sphere by the alternate construction of Ivanics, Stipsicz and Szabó
[
ISS19a
]
,
[
ISS19b
]
.

Remark 2.12
.

This analytic construction of
\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})
agrees with the algebraic one in
[
Yok93
]
, and hence in particular, the map
\pi_{\bm{\alpha}}

is just the usual one. Thus, if
({\mathcal{E}},\varphi)
is a parabolic Higgs bundle with a full flag
\{0\}\subset F_{p}\subset E_{p}
at each
p\in D
, then
the residue
\mathrm{Res}(\varphi)\Big|_{p}
in the eigenline
F_{p}
is the complex mass
m_{p}
.

2.6.
Hitchin map

The Hitchin map is defined on this extended bundle as

\mathrm{Hit}:\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})\to\mathcal{B}=H^{0}(K^{2}(2D)),\ \ (\mathcal{E},\varphi)\mapsto-\det\varphi,

and was proved by Yokogawa
[
Yok93
]
to be proper.

It is compatible with the Hitchin maps on each fiber

\mathrm{Hit}:\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})\to\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{m}).

This base is an affine space modeled on
H^{0}(K^{2}(D))
, which consists of elements in
H^{0}(K^{2}(2D))
which have residue
m_{i}^{2}
at
p_{i}
.
In particular,
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{0})=H^{0}(K^{2}(D))
. The spaces
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{m})
together as an affine bundle
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}\to\mathbb{C}^{4}
, cf.
[
CFW24
, Remark 5.6]
. Thus, the maps
\pi_{\bm{\alpha}}:\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})\to\mathbb{C}^{4}
and

\mathrm{Hit}:\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})\to H^{0}(K^{2}(2D))
factor through the map
\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})\to\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
.

We can make this even more concrete and give a trivialization
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{4}_{\mathbf{m}}\times\mathbb{C}_{\beta}
:

Proposition 2.13

(Hitchin Base)
.

Fix
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{C}^{4}
.
The Hitchin base consists of the space of quadratic differentials of the form

(2.4)

\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{m})=\left\{q=\frac{f_{\mathbf{m}}(z)+\beta z(z-1)(z-p_{0})}{z^{2}(z-1)^{2}(z-p_{0})^{2}}\mathrm{d}z^{2},\ \ \beta\in\mathbb{C}\right\}

where

\displaystyle f_{\mathbf{m}}(z):=m_{\infty}^{2}z^{4}+\frac{1}{3}(-m_{0}^{2}+2m_{1}^{2}-4m_{\infty}^{2}-m_{p_{0}}^{2}-m_{0}^{2}p_{0}-m_{1}^{2}p_{0}-4m_{\infty}^{2}p_{0}+2m_{p}^{2}p_{0})z^{3}+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}(m_{0}^{2}+m_{1}^{2}+m_{\infty}^{2}+m_{p_{0}}^{2}+5m_{0}^{2}p_{0}-4m_{1}^{2}p_{0}+5m_{\infty}^{2}p_{0}-4m_{p_{0}}^{2}p_{0}+m_{0}^{2}p_{0}^{2}+m_{1}^{2}p_{0}^{2}+m_{\infty}^{2}p_{0}^{2}+m_{p_{0}}^{2}p_{0}^{2})z^{2}+

\displaystyle-\frac{p_{0}}{3}(4m_{0}^{2}+m_{1}^{2}+m_{\infty}^{2}-2m_{p_{0}}^{2}+4m_{0}^{2}p_{0}-2m_{1}^{2}p_{0}+m_{\infty}^{2}p_{0}+m_{p_{0}}^{2}p_{0})z+m_{0}^{2}p_{0}^{2}.

Proof.

The fact that
q\in H^{0}(K^{2}(2D))
implies that
q=\frac{f(z)}{z^{2}(z-1)^{2}(z-p_{0})^{2}}dz^{2}
for some quartic polynomial
f=\sum_{j=0}^{4}f_{j}z^{j}
.
The condition on the residues yields

(2.5)

m_{0}^{2}=\frac{f(0)}{p_{0}^{2}},\quad m_{1}^{2}=\frac{f(1)}{(1-p_{0})^{2}},\quad m_{p_{0}}^{2}=\frac{f(p_{0})}{p_{0}^{2}(p_{0}-1)^{2}},\quad m_{\infty}^{2}=f_{4}.

The particular polynomial
f_{\mathbf{m}}(z)
above is the unique quartic polynomial satisfying these constraints for which the
\beta^{5}
-coefficient in
the (
6^{\mathrm{th}}
order in
\beta
) discriminant polynomial of
f_{\mathbf{m}}(z)+\beta z(z-1)(z-p_{0})
with respect to
z
vanishes.
∎

Remark 2.14
.

This quartic polynomial
f_{\mathbf{m}}(z)
has the following geometric significance. The fiber
\mathrm{Hit}^{-1}(q)\subset:\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})\to\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{m})

is smooth if and only if
q\in H^{0}(K^{2}(2D))
has simple zeros, which is the same as the nonvanishing of the discriminant of
f_{\mathbf{m}}
. The fibers at the
zeroes of this discriminant are singular. The negative of the coefficient of
\beta^{5}
in this discriminant is the sum of these six zeroes, counted
with multiplicity. So in other words,
f_{\mathbf{m}}
is normalized so that the center of mass of the locations of the singular fibers occurs at
\beta=0
.

2.7.
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action and Białynicki–Birula stratification

In this section, we will discuss the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
action on
\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})
and its accompanying Białynicki–Birula stratification.

Białynicki–Birula stratifications for complex manifolds equipped with holomorphic
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
actions are
provided by Białynicki–Birula
[
BB73
,
BB74
]
, Carrell and Sommese
[
CS79
]
, Kirwan
[
Kir88
]
, and Yang
[
Yan08
]
, under additional hypotheses, e.g. (1) the manifold is a smooth algebraic variety
[
BB73
,
BB74
]
, (2) the manifold is compact Kähler
[
CS79
,
Kir88
]
, or (3), in the non-compact Kähler setting under the assumption that the circle action
S^{1}\subset\mathbb{C}^{\times}
admits a Hamiltonian moment map that is proper and bounded below, such that the number of connected components of the fixed point set is finite
[
Yan08
]
.

In the case of non-parabolic (i.e. ordinary) Higgs bundles, there is a
\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{\zeta}
-action on the moduli space given by

[({\mathcal{E}},\varphi)]\mapsto[({\mathcal{E}},\zeta\varphi)].

In this case, the Hitchin moduli spaces satisfy the hypotheses
[
Yan08
]
, and hence have Białynicki–Birula stratifications.
Since the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action rescales the Higgs field, note that in its extension to parabolic Higgs bundles, the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action will rescale the complex masses by
m_{p}\mapsto\zeta m_{p}
. Consequently, the Hitchin moduli space
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
has a
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action precisely when
\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{0}
. (Again, in this case,
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{0})
satisfies the hypotheses in
[
Yan08
]
.) The extended moduli space
\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})
admits a
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action, but it does not satisfy the hypotheses in
[
Yan08
]
because it is not Kähler. Similarly, it does not satisfy the hypotheses of
[
Fee22
]
which make use of a non-degenerate (but not necessarily closed)
2
-form
\omega
. Consequently, we can’t use any of powerful results about Białynicki–Birula stratifications for smooth complex manifolds off the shelf.

We begin with the standard
Białynicki–Birula decomposition of non-singular semi-projective varieties, e.g. see the summary of results in
[
HRV13
, §1.2]
.
The moduli space
\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})
as constructed by Yokogawa is a quasi-projective variety. Since this
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-fixed point set is projective (we will soon see that it is the union of
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
and four disjoint points) and all
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-limits exist inside of
\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})
, the space
\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})
is semi-projective. When
\bm{\alpha}
is generic, it is non-singular. Consequently, it has a Białynicki–Birula stratification.

In particular, by the Białynicki
-
-Birula theory for non-singular semi
-
projective varieties, (1) there is a decomposition of

\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})=\bigsqcup_{a\in\pi_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})^{\mathbb{C}^{\times}})}\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})_{a}^{+};\qquad\qquad\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})_{a}^{+}=\{x\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})|\lim_{t\to 0}t\cdot x\in a\}

by locally closed subvarieties, (2) when
\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})
is smooth, for each fixed component
a
,

\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})_{a}^{+}\to a

is a locally trivial affine bundle, (3) the Białynicki
-
-Birula strata are ordered by a partial order determined by weights, and

\overline{\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})_{a}^{+}}\subset\bigcup_{a^{\prime}\leq a}\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})_{a^{\prime}}^{+}.

In particular, since
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{0})\subset\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})
contains all the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-fixed points and is Kähler, this partial ordering is from the upwards Morse flow. In this case, the central
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
is higher than each of the four exterior
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-points; there is no comparison between the four exterior
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-fixed points. As a result, for each exterior
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-fixed point, the corresponding stratum is closed. We will call these
generalized Hitchin sections
.
7
7

7

In fact, over the extended Hitchin base
\hat{\mathcal{B}}
, these really are sections.
The strata for the central
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
is open, but becomes closed once adds in the generalized Hitchin sections.

Note that since we are interested in the analytically defined moduli space rather than the algebraically defined moduli space in
[
Yok93
]
, there is some accompanying subtlety involving the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-limits addressed in

[
CFW24
, Proposition 4.1]

8
8

8

Moreover, note that Collier-Fredrickson-Wentworth extend this and show that
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-limit exists on the larger space of logarithmic
\lambda
-connections, of which
\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})
is the fiber over
\lambda=0
[
CFW24
, Proposition 2.11]
.
.
We can upgrade the above statements to statements about smooth submanifolds in
\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})
using the slices in
[
CFW24
]
.
Moreover, as in
[
CFW24
, Proposition 4.7]
, these slices are compatible with the projection of the framed analytic moduli space to the residues of
\varphi
at
p\in D
.
Consequently, the Białynicki–Birula stratification of
\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bm{\alpha})
induces a stratification of each
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
by intersecting the strata with it.

We briefly discuss the structure of the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-fixed point set. The
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-fixed points are known to be
systems of Hodge bundles

[
Sim87
]
. The description of these Hodge bundles depends on
\bm{\alpha}
; this chamber-dependence is described in detail in Section
3.2
, and then we can address this stratification more explicitly.
All the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-fixed points lie in the fiber defined by
q=-\det\varphi=0
inside of the strongly parabolic moduli space
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{0})
.
The preimage of
q=0
in
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{0})
is shown in Figure
2.2
; it consists of five spheres in an affine
D_{4}
-configuration.
Every point of the central sphere is
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-fixed point.
Each of the four exterior spheres
\mathbb{CP}^{1}_{p}
for
p\in D
has exactly
two

\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-fixed points which we label
q_{p}^{\pm}
. We call the attaching point between exterior sphere and central sphere the
interior fixed point
(or
wobbly point
following Simpson) and denote it
q_{p}^{-}
. We call the other one the
exterior fixed point
and denote it
q_{p}^{+}
.

Figure 2.2.

\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-fixed points are shown in red

Consequently, the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-fixed point set consists of five components when
\bm{\alpha}
is generic: a copy of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
and four additional points.

3.
Chamber structure of stability conditions, the nilpotent cone and consistent homology bases

In this section we review the chamber structure for the space of real mass parameters
\bm{\alpha}
for which the stability condition holds when the complex mass
parameters
\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{0}
. This leads to an explicit description and parametrization of the nilpotent cone in these regimes.

Recall from Definition
2.1
, that the parabolic data
(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})\in((0,\tfrac{1}{2})\times\mathbb{C})^{4}
is called generic if all semistable Higgs bundles with
these parameters are stable. The set of all generic parameters
\mathcal{R}
is the complement of a collection of real-codimension three affine subspaces.
The intersection of these affine subspaces with
\{(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m}):\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{0}\}
is a set of affine hyperplanes which divide
(0,\tfrac{1}{2})^{4}
into chambers.

We first review this chamber structure in Section
3.1
, following
[
Men22
]
, and then in Section
3.2
use the combinatorial
data associated to these chambers to characterize the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-fixed points. Finally, Section
3.3
contains a full description of the nilpotent cone,
i.e., the fiber over the quadratic differential
q=0
, in
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{0})
as an affine
D_{4}
pattern of five spheres, which
constitute a distinguished basis for
H_{2}(\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{0}),\mathbb{Z})
.

We pause briefly to explain how these results are used later. This distinguished homology bases for the strongly parabolic moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{0})

are used to obtain homology bases for
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{m})
. The integrals of
\omega_{I}
over such a homology basis
when
\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{0}
are calculated using the explicit description of the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-fixed points in Section
3.2
, but we then show that these
integrals are independent of
\mathbf{m}
. On the other hand, we reduce the calculation of the integrals of
\Omega_{I}
over this distinguished basis
to the intersection numbers of the four outer spheres in the
D_{4}
arrangement and four other
2
-cycles related to the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-flow.

For simplicity below, we simply label the distinct points
0,1,p_{0},\infty
by
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}
.

3.1.
Chamber Structure

We now recall Meneses’
[
Men22
]
description of the chamber structure of the space of
real mass parameters
(0,\frac{1}{2})^{4}
.

The starting point is the Biswas polytope, which by definition is the subset of parabolic weights which admit stable parabolic bundles. When
C=\mathbb{CP}^{1}
,
the cube
(0,1/2)^{4}
is divided into 24 chambers by 12 hyperplanes. Amongst these, the
8
“exterior” chambers lie outside the Biswas polytope
while the 16 “interior” chambers lie inside it. These have slightly different combinatorial descriptions.

3.1.1.
Interior chambers

The interior chambers are in bijective correspondence with the
even partition sets
of
\{1,2,3,4\}
as
defined by
[
Men22
, Definition 1]
:

Definition 3.1
.

Consider the set of all decompositions
I\sqcup I^{c}=\{1,2,3,4\}
where
I
has even cardinality. This is a subset of the power set
2^{\{1,2,3,4\}}

consisting of those
I\subset\{1,2,3,4\}
such that either
I
or its complement
I^{c}
lie in
\mathtt{E}:=\{\emptyset,\{1,2\},\{1,3\},\{1,4\}\}
.
An
